A comparison of our work with early works is briefly summarised in Table
1
and
2
.
The rest of the paper is organised as follows. Section
2
specifies the class of hypo-elliptic SDEs of relevance for this work, with reference to Hörmander’s condition. Section
3

revisits the correct (in terms of its statistical properties) discretisation scheme for class
(
Hypo-I
)
and introduces the one for (
Hypo-II
).
Section
4
provides our core analytical results of asymptotic consistency and normality for the statistical estimates obtained via the new scheme, in a complete observation setting. All proofs are collected in an Appendix. We present case studies showcasing the emergence of bias when
standard alternative schemes are called upon or when finite-differences are used to impute unobserved components (a common practice in applications).
Under the correct schemes shown here for classes (
Hypo-I
) and (
Hypo-II
), we set up a simple Kalman filter for fitting a non-linear sub-class of models commonly arising in applications (we term these
conditional Gaussian non-linear systems
) in the practical partial observation setting.
Section
5
presents numerical studies, for the partial observation regime, both for simple models and ones relevant to real applications, within class (
Hypo-II
). The code used in the numerical studies is available at https://github.com/YugaIgu/calibration-hypoSDEs.
We finish with some conclusions in Section
6
.

Notation.

For the highly degenerate class (
Hypo-II
), to establish a common notation with (
Hypo-I
), we use the argument
\displaystyle x_{S}=(x_{S_{1}},x_{S_{2}})
and also set:

\displaystyle\displaystyle X_{S,t}=\bigl[X_{S_{1},t}^{\top},X_{S_{2},t}^{\top}\bigr]^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{S}},\quad N_{S}=N_{S_{1}}+N_{S_{2}};

\displaystyle\displaystyle\beta_{S}=\bigl[\beta_{S_{1}}^{\top},\beta_{S_{2}}^{\top}\bigr]^{\top}\in\Theta_{\beta_{S}},\quad\Theta_{\beta_{S}}=\Theta_{\beta_{S_{1}}}\times\Theta_{\beta_{S_{2}}},\quad N_{\beta_{S}}=N_{\beta_{S_{1}}}+N_{\beta_{S_{2}}};

\displaystyle\displaystyle V_{S,0}(x,\beta_{S})=\bigl[V_{S_{1},0}(x_{S},\beta_{S_{1}})^{\top},\;V_{S_{2},0}(x,\beta_{S_{2}})^{\top}\bigr]^{\top}.

For
\displaystyle x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
and
\displaystyle\theta=(\beta_{S},\beta_{R},\sigma)\in\Theta
,
we write

\displaystyle\displaystyle V_{0}(x,\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\left[V_{S,0}(x,\beta_{S})^{\top},\;V_{R,0}(x,\beta_{R})^{\top}\right]^{\top};

(3)

\displaystyle\displaystyle V_{j}(x,\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\left[\mathbf{0}_{N_{S}}^{\top},\;V_{R,j}(x,\sigma)^{\top}\right]^{\top},\qquad 1\leq j\leq d.

For
\displaystyle\varphi(\cdot,\theta):\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}
,
\displaystyle\theta\in\Theta
, bounded up to 2nd order derivatives, we define the differential operators
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{j},\;1\leq j\leq d
, as:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\varphi(x,\theta)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}V_{0}^{i}(x,\theta)\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x_{i}}(x,\theta)+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i_{1},i_{2}=1}^{N}\sum_{k=1}^{d}V_{k}^{i_{1}}(x,\theta)V_{k}^{i_{2}}(x,\theta)\frac{\partial^{2}\varphi}{\partial x_{i_{1}}\partial x_{i_{2}}}(x,\theta);

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{j}\varphi(x,\theta)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}V_{j}^{i}(x,\theta)\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x_{i}}(x,\theta),\ \ 1\leq j\leq d,

for
\displaystyle(x,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\Theta
.
Application of the above differential operators is extended to vector-valued functions in the apparent way, via separate consideration of each scalar component.
We denote the probability law of the process
\displaystyle\{X_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
under a parameter
\displaystyle\theta\in\Theta
as
\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\theta}
, and we write

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}},\ \ \xrightarrow{\mathcal{L}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}

for convergence in probability and distribution, respectively, under the true parameter
\displaystyle\theta^{\dagger}
. We write the expectation under the probability law
\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\theta}
as
\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\theta}
to emphasise the dependence on
\displaystyle\theta\in\Theta
. For
\displaystyle u\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\displaystyle n\in\mathbb{N}
and the multi-index
\displaystyle\alpha\in\{1,\ldots,n\}^{l}
,
\displaystyle l\in\mathbb{N}
, we define
\displaystyle\textstyle{\partial^{u}_{\alpha}=\partial^{l}/\partial u_{\alpha_{1}}\cdots\partial u_{\alpha_{l}}}
, i.e. an operator acting on maps
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
, and then extended, by separate application on each co-ordinate, on maps
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
,
\displaystyle m\in\mathbb{N}
. We denote by

\displaystyle C_{p}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}}\times\Theta;\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}})
,
\displaystyle n_{1},n_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
,
the space of functions
\displaystyle f:\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}}\times\Theta\to\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}}
such that
\displaystyle f(x,\theta)
is infinitely differentiable w.r.t.
\displaystyle x\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}}
for all
\displaystyle\theta\in\Theta
, and for any
\displaystyle\alpha\in\{1,\ldots,n_{1}\}^{\ell},\,\ell\geq 0
,
\displaystyle\partial_{x}^{\alpha}f(x,\theta)
is of polynomial growth in
\displaystyle x\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}}
uniformly in
\displaystyle\theta\in\Theta
. For
\displaystyle x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
, we write
\displaystyle\textstyle\langle x,y\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}y_{i}
and
\displaystyle\|x\|=\sqrt{\langle x,x\rangle}
.

2
Hypo-Elliptic SDEs

We fully specify the classes of SDEs of interest in (
Hypo-I
) and (
Hypo-II
), by providing, in each case, appropriate conditions on the collection of functionals

\displaystyle\{V_{0},V_{1},\ldots,V_{d}\}
, motivated by modelling considerations and the existence of a Lebesgue density for the SDE transition dynamics.
We illustrate later on that the imposed conditions suffice so that the locally Gaussian scheme for
\displaystyle X_{t}
in (
Hypo-II
) we put forward in this paper is non-degenerate.

2.1
Hörmander’s Condition

We quickly review the definition of Hörmander’s condition.
Consider the class of SDEs with the general form in (
1
). We define

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\tilde{V}_{0}(x,\theta)=V_{0}(x,\theta)-\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{d}\mathcal{L}_{k}V_{k}(x,\theta)},\quad(x,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\Theta.

From standard properties of Itô’s processes,
\displaystyle\tilde{V}_{0}
is the drift function of (
1
) when written as a Stratonovich-type SDE. The functionals
\displaystyle\{\tilde{V}_{0},\ldots,V_{d}\}
of the Stratonovich SDE can be corresponded to differential operators, the latter applying on mappings on
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
and giving as outcome mappings, again, on the same spaces. In particular, we have:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\tilde{V}_{0}\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tilde{V}_{0}^{i}(x){\partial_{x_{i}}},\qquad V_{k}\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{N}{V}_{k}^{i}(x){\partial_{x_{i}}},\quad 1\leq k\leq d.

Without confusion, we use the same notation both for the SDE
functionals and the corresponding differential operators.
Parameter
\displaystyle\theta
is removed from the expressions for simplicity. For two functionals (equivalently, differential operators) as above,

\displaystyle\textstyle{W=\sum_{i=1}^{N}W^{i}(x)\partial_{x_{i}}}
and
\displaystyle\textstyle{Z=\sum_{i=1}^{N}Z^{i}(x)\partial_{x_{i}}}
, the Lie bracket is defined as

\displaystyle\displaystyle[W,Z]=W\,Z-Z\,W,

that is, for a given
\displaystyle x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle[W,Z](x)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bigl\{W^{i}(x)\partial_{x_{i}}Z(x)-Z^{i}(x)\partial_{x_{i}}W(x)\bigr\}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}.

We introduce the collections of functionals

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathscr{H}_{0}=\big\{V_{1},\ldots,V_{d}\big\},\qquad\mathscr{H}_{k}=\Big\{\big\{\,[\tilde{V}_{0},V],[V_{r},V]\,\big\}:V\in\mathscr{H}_{k-1},\,1\leq r\leq d\Big\},\quad k\geq 1.

Then, Hörmander’s condition is stated as follows:

Definition
.

Hörmander’s condition is said to hold at a point
\displaystyle x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
if there exists
\displaystyle M\geq 0
such that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathrm{span}\big\{V(x):V\in\mathscr{H}_{j},\ \ 0\leq j\leq M\big\}=\mathbb{R}^{N}.

(4)

Hörmander’s condition implies that for any
\displaystyle t>0
and an initial condition
\displaystyle X_{0}=x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
, the law of
\displaystyle X_{t}
is absolutely continuous w.r.t. the Lebesgue measure. Also, if the coefficients of the SDE are infinitely-times differentiable, with partial derivatives of all orders being bounded, then the Lebesgue density is smooth, see, e.g.,
[
28
,
29
]
. When Hörmander’s condition holds with
\displaystyle M=0
, the condition is typically referred to as
strong
Hörmander’s condition and is equivalent to the SDE being an elliptic one. It is easy to see that in our setting of a degenerate diffusion matrix, Hörmander’s condition cannot hold with
\displaystyle M=0
. If (
4
) holds with
\displaystyle M\geq 1
, then the term
weak
Hörmander’s condition is often used, see e.g.
[
30
]
. Furthermore, if (
4
) holds uniformly in the initial point
\displaystyle x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
, we have the
uniform
Hörmander’s condition, which is invoked frequently in the literature, see e.g.
[
2
,
4
]
.
Precisely, the uniform Hörmander’s condition is stated as follows.
There exists an integer
\displaystyle M\geq 0
such that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}}\ \inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\mathrm{s.t.}\\
\|\xi\|=1\end{subarray}}\,\sum_{j=0}^{M}\sum_{V\in\mathscr{H}_{j}}\Bigl\langle V(x),\,\xi\Bigr\rangle^{2}>0.

(5)

To establish a statistical theory for models (
Hypo-I
) and (
Hypo-II
), we work below with assumptions associated with the uniform Hörmander’s condition.

2.2
Diffusion Classes (
Hypo-I
) and (
Hypo-II
)

We now set up separate conditions for the
SDEs in classes (
Hypo-I
) and (
Hypo-II
). These will make use of the drift function,

\displaystyle\tilde{V}_{0}=\tilde{V}_{0}(x,\theta)
, of the Stratonovich version of the SDEs.
For
\displaystyle 1\leq j\leq k\leq N
, we define the projection operator
\displaystyle\mathrm{proj}_{j,k}:\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}^{k-j+1}
as

\displaystyle\displaystyle x=\bigl[x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}\bigr]^{\top}\mapsto\mathrm{proj}_{j,k}(x)=\bigl[x_{j},\ldots,x_{k}\bigr]^{\top}.

We also introduce:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}_{0}=\mathscr{H}_{0},\qquad\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}_{1}=\Bigl\{[\widetilde{V}_{0},V]:\,V\in\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}_{0}\,\Bigr\}\qquad\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}_{2}=\Bigl\{\bigl[\widetilde{V}_{0},[\widetilde{V}_{0},V]\bigr]:\,V\in\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}_{0}\,\Bigr\}.

For (
Hypo-I
) and (
Hypo-II
), we assign the following conditions to fully specify the structure of the corresponding degenerate system of SDEs.

Condition (H)

(Classes of SDEs)
.

I.

For class (
Hypo-I
), it holds that:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\inf_{(x,\sigma)\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\Theta_{\sigma}}\ \inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{R}}\\
\mathrm{s.t.}\,\|\xi\|=1\end{subarray}}\sum_{V\in\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}_{0}}\Bigl\langle\mathrm{proj}_{N_{S}+1,N}\bigl\{V(x,\theta)\bigr\},\ \xi\Bigr\rangle^{2}=\inf_{(x,\sigma)\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\Theta_{\sigma}}\ \inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{R}}\\
\mathrm{s.t.}\,\|\xi\|=1\end{subarray}}\sum_{k=1}^{d}\Bigl\langle V_{R,k}(x,\sigma),\ \xi\Bigr\rangle^{2}>0;

\displaystyle\displaystyle\inf_{(x,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\Theta}\ \inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\\
\mathrm{s.t.}\,\|\xi\|=1\end{subarray}}\sum_{j=0,1}\sum_{V\in\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}_{j}}\Bigl\langle V(x,\theta),\ \xi\Bigr\rangle^{2}>0.

(6)

II.

In the case of class (
Hypo-II
), it holds that:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\inf_{(x,\sigma)\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\Theta_{\sigma}}\ \inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{R}}\\
\mathrm{s.t.}\,\|\xi\|=1\end{subarray}}\sum_{V\in\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}_{0}}\Bigl\langle\mathrm{proj}_{N_{S}+1,N}\bigl\{V(x,\theta)\bigr\},\ \xi\Bigr\rangle^{2}=\inf_{(x,\sigma)\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\Theta_{\sigma}}\ \inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{R}}\\
\mathrm{s.t.}\,\|\xi\|=1\end{subarray}}\sum_{k=1}^{d}\Bigl\langle V_{R,k}(x,\sigma),\ \xi\Bigr\rangle^{2}>0;

\displaystyle\displaystyle\inf_{(x,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\Theta}\ \inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{S_{2}}+N_{R}}\\
\mathrm{s.t.}\,\|\xi\|=1\end{subarray}}\sum_{j=0,1}\sum_{V\in\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}_{j}}\Bigl\langle\mathrm{proj}_{N_{S_{1}}+1,N}\bigl\{V(x,\theta)\bigr\},\ \xi\Bigr\rangle^{2}>0;

\displaystyle\displaystyle\inf_{(x,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\Theta}\ \inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\\
\mathrm{s.t.}\,\|\xi\|=1\end{subarray}}\sum_{j=0,1,2}\sum_{V\in\widetilde{\mathscr{H}}_{j}}\Bigl\langle V(x,\theta),\ \xi\Bigr\rangle^{2}>0.

Note that the uniform Hörmander’s condition holds for (
Hypo-I
) and (
Hypo-II
) under
(H)
-I and
(H)
-II (for
\displaystyle M=1
and
\displaystyle M=2
) respectively.

Remark 1
.

Conditions
(H)
-I and
(H)
-II
separate classes (
Hypo-I
) and (
Hypo-II
).
In particular, the top equation in both
(H)
-I,
(H)
-II implies that the diffusion matrix of the rough component is of full rank, thus
\displaystyle X_{R,t}
acquires the roughness of an elliptic SDE.
The second equation in
(H)
-I,
(H)
-II ensures that all coordinates of component
\displaystyle X_{S,t}
and
\displaystyle X_{S_{2},t}
, respectively, possess the same smoothness as integrals of elliptic SDEs, that is, contain a Gaussian noise with the size of
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(\Delta_{n}^{3/2})
.
Finally, the third equation in
(H)
-II implies that component
\displaystyle X_{S_{1},t}
has the smoothness of second integrals of elliptic SDEs, i.e., contain a Gaussian noise of size
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(\Delta_{n}^{5/2})
. Note, e.g., that (
6
) will not hold for (
Hypo-II
), since for the highly degenerate case, due to the drift function of the upper-most component
\displaystyle X_{S_{1},t}
not involving
\displaystyle X_{R,t}
,
we have

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathrm{proj}_{1,N_{S_{1}}}[\tilde{V}_{0},V_{k}]=\mathbf{0}_{N_{S_{1}}},\quad 1\leq k\leq d.

To check this latter equation, notice that for both (
Hypo-I
) and (
Hypo-II
) we have: i)
\displaystyle V_{0}
and
\displaystyle\tilde{V}_{0}
coincide on the smooth coordinates; ii)
\displaystyle\tilde{V}_{0}V_{k}
is zero on the smooth coordinates. Then, for (
Hypo-II
) we additionally have that
\displaystyle V_{k}\tilde{V}_{0}
is zero on the upper-most
\displaystyle N_{S_{1}}
coordinates due to the particular choice of
\displaystyle V_{0}=V_{0}(x_{S_{1}},x_{S_{2}})
.

Remark 2
.

We introduced conditions
(H)
-I & II upon functionals
\displaystyle\{V_{0},V_{1},\ldots,V_{d}\}
, so that
the two classes of SDEs, (
Hypo-I
) and (
Hypo-II
), possess sufficient structure to allow for their intended use for the modelling objectives in mind.
It turns out that the exact same conditions
(H)
-I & II play a key role so that the locally Gaussian approximation for
\displaystyle X_{t_{i+1}}|X_{t_{i}}=x
written down later in Section
3
are
well-defined with a positive definite covariance matrix for all
\displaystyle(x,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\Theta
.

2.2.1
An Example for
(H)
-I

We provide an example for
(H)
-I via the following bivariate underdamped Langevin equation:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{aligned} dq_{t}&=p_{t}dt;\\[5.69046pt]
dp_{t}&=\bigl(-U^{\prime}(q_{t})-\gamma p_{t}\bigr)dt+\sigma dB_{1,t},\end{aligned}

(7)

where
\displaystyle U:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
,
\displaystyle\gamma>0
and
\displaystyle\sigma>0
. We write
\displaystyle x=(q,p)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
\displaystyle\theta=(\gamma,\sigma)\in\Theta
, where
\displaystyle\Theta
is an appropriate compact space. We have:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\widetilde{V}_{0}=p\partial_{q}+(-U^{\prime}(q)-\gamma p)\partial_{p},\qquad V_{1}=\sigma\partial_{p},\qquad[\widetilde{V}_{0},V_{1}]=-\sigma\partial_{q}+\gamma\sigma\partial_{p},

and then

\displaystyle\displaystyle V_{1}(x,\sigma)=\begin{bmatrix}0\\
V_{R,1}(x,\sigma)\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}0\\
\sigma\end{bmatrix},\qquad[\widetilde{V}_{0},V_{1}](x,\theta)=\begin{bmatrix}-\sigma\\
\gamma\sigma\end{bmatrix}.

Thus, SDE (
7
) satisfies condition
(H)
-I and lies within the framework of class (
Hypo-I
).

2.2.2
An Example for
(H)
-II

We provide an example for
(H)
-II via the following three-dimensional hypo-elliptic diffusion motivated from model class (
QGLE-II
):

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{aligned} dq_{t}&=p_{t}dt;\\[5.69046pt]
dp_{t}&=\bigl(-U^{\prime}(q_{t})+\lambda s_{t}\bigr)dt;\\[2.84544pt]
ds_{t}&=\bigl(-\lambda p_{t}-\alpha s_{t}\bigr)dt+\sigma dB_{1,t},\end{aligned}

(8)

where
\displaystyle U:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
,
\displaystyle\alpha>0
,
\displaystyle\sigma>0
and
\displaystyle\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
.
Notice that the drift function of the positional component
\displaystyle q_{t}
is independent of the rough component
\displaystyle s_{t}
.
In this case, for
\displaystyle x=(p,q,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
\displaystyle\theta=(\lambda,\alpha,\sigma)\in\Theta
with some appropriate compact space
\displaystyle\Theta
, we have:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\tilde{V}_{0}=p\,\partial_{q}+\bigl(-U^{\prime}(q)+\lambda s\bigr)\partial_{p}+\bigl(-\lambda p-\alpha s\bigr)\partial_{s},\qquad V_{1}=\sigma\partial_{s},

\displaystyle\displaystyle[\tilde{V}_{0},V_{1}]=-\lambda\sigma\partial_{p}+\alpha\sigma\partial_{s},\qquad\bigl[\tilde{V}_{0},[\tilde{V}_{0},V_{1}]\bigr]=\lambda\sigma\partial_{q}-\lambda\alpha\sigma\partial_{p}+\sigma(-\lambda^{2}+\alpha^{2})\partial_{s}.

We obtain:

\displaystyle\displaystyle V_{1}(x,\sigma)=\begin{bmatrix}0\\
0\\
V_{R}(x,\sigma)\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}0\\
0\\
\sigma\end{bmatrix},\qquad[\widetilde{V}_{0},V_{1}](x,\theta)=\begin{bmatrix}0\\
-\lambda\sigma\\
\alpha\sigma\end{bmatrix},\qquad\bigl[\widetilde{V}_{0},[\widetilde{V}_{0},V_{1}]\bigr](x,\theta)=\begin{bmatrix}\lambda\sigma\\
-\lambda\alpha\sigma\\
\sigma(-\lambda^{2}+\alpha^{2})\end{bmatrix}.

Thus, SDE (
8
) satisfies condition
(H)
-II and lies within the framework of class (
Hypo-II
).

3
Time-Discretisation of Hypo-Elliptic SDEs

We discuss time-discretisation schemes for hypo-elliptic diffusions within classes (
Hypo-I
) and (
Hypo-II
), with the focus being on the performance of the schemes for the purposes of parametric inference. We set up the context by reviewing schemes proposed in literature for class (
Hypo-I
).
We then propose a new scheme for the highly degenerate diffusion class (
Hypo-II
) that will later be proven to possess desirable statistical properties.
Hereafter, to distinguish among the two classes of SDEs, we use the notation
\displaystyle\textstyle{X_{t}^{(\mathrm{I})}}
and
\displaystyle\textstyle{X_{t}^{(\mathrm{II})}}
for processes in (
Hypo-I
) and (
Hypo-II
), respectively.

3.1
Time-Discretisation of (
Hypo-I
)
– Brief Review

We review relevant schemes for the hypo-elliptic class (
Hypo-I
) used in the literature.
First, the classical
Euler-Maruyama
scheme is defined as follows, for
\displaystyle 0\leq i\leq n
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{aligned} X_{S,i+1}^{\mathrm{EM},(\mathrm{I})}&=X_{S,i}^{\mathrm{EM},(\mathrm{I})}+V_{S,0}\bigl(X_{i}^{\mathrm{EM},(\mathrm{I})},\beta_{S}\bigr)\Delta_{n};\\
X_{R,i+1}^{\mathrm{EM},(\mathrm{I})}&=X_{R,i}^{\mathrm{EM},(\mathrm{I})}+V_{R,0}\bigl(X_{i}^{\mathrm{EM},(\mathrm{I})},\beta_{R}\bigr)\Delta_{n}+\sum_{j=1}^{d}V_{R,j}\bigl(X_{i}^{\mathrm{EM},(\mathrm{I})},\sigma\bigr)\times\bigl(B_{j,i+1}-B_{j,i}\bigr),\end{aligned}

with subscript
\displaystyle i
in
\displaystyle X_{i}^{\mathrm{EM},(\mathrm{I})}
and
\displaystyle B_{j,i}
indicating the time instance
\displaystyle t_{i}=i\Delta_{n}
. The approximation of the smooth component does not involve noise, thus the Euler-Maruyama scheme is degenerate.
[
32
]
studied some example bivariate SDEs with drift function
\displaystyle V_{S,0}(x,\beta_{S}\bigr)=x_{R}
, and showed that use of the Euler-Maruyama scheme in the high-frequency partial observation regime, where only the smooth component
\displaystyle X_{S,t}
is observed, induces bias in parameter estimates. Note that in this setting the unobserved component
\displaystyle X_{R,t}
is estimated via a
finite-difference approach, i.e.,

\displaystyle\displaystyle X_{R,i}^{\,(\mathrm{I})}\approx\hat{X}_{R,i}^{\,(\mathrm{I})}=\frac{X_{S,i+1}^{\,(\mathrm{I})}-X_{S,i}^{\,(\mathrm{I})}}{\Delta_{n}},

and such an imputation is a main cause for the presence of bias in the estimation of parameter
\displaystyle\sigma
. To sidestep the above issue,
[
32
]
proposed the following conditionally Gaussian scheme, for
\displaystyle 0\leq i\leq n
:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \widetilde{X}_{S,i+1}^{\,(\mathrm{I})}&=\widetilde{X}_{S,i}^{\,(\mathrm{I})}+V_{S,0}\bigl(\widetilde{X}_{i}^{\,(\mathrm{I})},\beta_{S}\bigr)\Delta_{n}+\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathcal{L}_{j}V_{S,0}(\widetilde{X}_{i}^{\,(\mathrm{I})},\theta)\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\int_{t_{i}}^{u}dB_{j,v}du;\\
\widetilde{X}_{R,i+1}^{\,(\mathrm{I})}&=\widetilde{X}_{R,i}^{\,(\mathrm{I})}+V_{R,0}\bigl(\widetilde{X}_{i}^{\,(\mathrm{I})},\beta_{R}\bigr)\Delta_{n}+\sum_{j=1}^{d}V_{R,j}\bigl(\widetilde{X}_{i}^{\,(\mathrm{I})},\sigma\bigr)\times\bigl(B_{j,i+1}-B_{j,i}\bigr).\end{aligned}

(9)

Note that now the smooth component
\displaystyle\widetilde{X}_{S,i+1}^{\,(\mathrm{I})}
involves Gaussian noise after application of an Itô-Taylor expansion for
\displaystyle\textstyle V_{S,0}(X_{t},\beta_{S})
. Under condition
(H)
-I,
\displaystyle(\widetilde{X}^{\,(\mathrm{I})}_{S,i+1},\widetilde{X}_{R,i+1}^{\,(\mathrm{I})})
is conditionally Gaussian with an invertible covariance matrix. Then,
[
32
]
utilised the well-posed likelihood of scheme (
9
) to estimate both the hidden paths of the rough components and the parameters via a Bayesian approach, namely Gibbs sampling. Under
a high-frequency observation setting, they empirically showed that the estimate of parameter
\displaystyle\sigma
is asymptotically unbiased,
but the estimator of the drift parameter
\displaystyle\beta_{R}
based on scheme (
9
) suffers from bias even in the complete observation regime.

[
14
]
introduced the ‘local Gaussian’ scheme, where, for
\displaystyle 0\leq i\leq n
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \bar{X}_{S,i+1}^{\,(\mathrm{I})}&=\bar{X}_{S,i}^{\,(\mathrm{I})}+V_{S,0}\bigl(\bar{X}_{i}^{\,(\mathrm{I})},\beta_{S}\bigr)\Delta_{n}+\tfrac{\Delta_{n}^{2}}{2}\mathcal{L}V_{S,0}\bigl(\bar{X}_{i}^{\,(\mathrm{I})},\theta\bigr)+\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathcal{L}_{j}V_{S,0}(\bar{X}_{i}^{\,(\mathrm{I})},\theta)\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\int_{t_{i}}^{u}dB_{j,v}du;\\
\bar{X}_{R,i+1}^{\,(\mathrm{I})}&=\bar{X}_{R,i}^{\,(\mathrm{I})}+V_{R,0}\bigl(\bar{X}_{i}^{\,(\mathrm{I})},\beta_{R}\bigr)\Delta_{n}+\sum_{j=1}^{d}V_{R,j}\bigl(\bar{X}_{i}^{\,(\mathrm{I})},\sigma\bigr)\times\bigl(B_{j,i+1}-B_{j,i}\bigr).\end{aligned}

(LG-I)

Compared to (
9
), scheme (
LG-I
) includes term
\displaystyle\textstyle{\Delta_{n}^{2}(\mathcal{L}V_{S,0})(\bar{X}^{\,(\mathrm{I})}_{i},\theta)}/2
in the smooth component.

[
14
]
illustrate the significance of this term for the purposes of parameter inference,
by proving asymptotic consistency and normality for the contrast estimator derived from the likelihood of the discretisation scheme (
LG-I
), in the high-frequency, complete observation regime, namely,
\displaystyle n\to\infty
,
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}\to 0
,
\displaystyle n\Delta_{n}\to\infty
, under the step-size condition
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}=o(n^{-1/2})
.

Remark 3
.

[
8
]
applied a strong 1.5 order scheme (
[
22
]
) to construct contrast estimators for class (
Hypo-I
) with
\displaystyle N_{S}=1
, under strong conditions for the diffusion matrix so that the scheme becomes conditionally Gaussian. Then, they provided two separate contrast functions for estimating
\displaystyle\beta_{S}
and
\displaystyle(\beta_{R},\sigma)
from the approximate Gaussian density for
\displaystyle X_{S}
and
\displaystyle X_{R}
, respectively, rather than the joint density. As noted in Remark 4.6 in
[
14
]
, the separate contrast functions result in a larger asymptotic variance for the estimation for
\displaystyle\beta_{S}
compared with the single contrast estimator defined via the joint density of rough and smooth components.

3.2
Time-Discretisation of (
Hypo-II
)

We propose a time-discretisation scheme for the second hypo-elliptic class (
Hypo-II
),
with desirable properties for the purposes of parameter inference.
The brief review of schemes for (
Hypo-I
) in the previous section suggests that the discretisation scheme for (
Hypo-II
) should satisfy the following two key criteria:

I.

The scheme should be conditionally non-degenerate, i.e., the law of
\displaystyle X_{t_{i+1}}
given
\displaystyle X_{t_{i}}
should admit a Lebesgue transition density for the full coordinates. This will allow to impute unobserved paths conditionally on observations without making use of bias-inducing finite-difference approximations.

II.

The scheme should involve deterministic terms
obtained from careful truncation of the stochastic Taylor expansion for the drift of the smooth component,
\displaystyle V_{S,0}(X_{t}^{\,(\mathrm{II})},\beta_{S})
, so that the contrast estimator corresponding to the scheme is asymptotically unbiased under the high-frequency, complete observation regime.

As for Criterion I, we will explain later in Section
4.2.1
that, indeed, use of a degenerate discretisation scheme or of
finite-differences to estimate hidden components induces a bias in the estimation of parameters. Based upon the above key criteria, we propose the following discretisation scheme for (
Hypo-II
):

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \bar{X}_{S_{1},i+1}^{\,\mathrm{(II)}}&=\mu_{S_{1}}\bigl(\Delta_{n},\bar{X}_{i}^{\,\mathrm{(II)}},\theta\bigr)+\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathcal{L}_{j}\mathcal{L}V_{S_{1},0}\bigl(\bar{X}_{i}^{\,\mathrm{(II)}},\theta\bigr)\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\int_{t_{i}}^{u}\int_{t_{i}}^{v}dB_{j,w}dvdu;\\[5.69046pt]
\bar{X}_{S_{2},i+1}^{\,\mathrm{(II)}}&=\mu_{S_{2}}\bigl(\Delta_{n},\bar{X}_{i}^{\,\mathrm{(II)}},\theta\bigr)+\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathcal{L}_{j}V_{S_{2},0}(\bar{X}_{i}^{\,\mathrm{(II)}},\theta)\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\int_{t_{i}}^{u}dB_{j,v}du;\\[5.69046pt]
\bar{X}_{R,i+1}^{\,\mathrm{(II)}}&=\mu_{R}\bigl(\Delta_{n},\bar{X}_{i}^{\,\mathrm{(II)}},\theta\bigr)+\sum_{j=1}^{d}V_{j}\bigl(\bar{X}_{i}^{\,\mathrm{(II)}},\sigma\bigr)\times\bigl(B_{j,i+1}-B_{j,i}\bigr),\end{aligned}

(LG-II)

where we have set, for
\displaystyle(\Delta,x,\theta)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\Theta
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}{\mu}_{S_{1}}(\Delta,x,\theta)\\[8.5359pt]
{\mu}_{S_{2}}(\Delta,x,\theta)\\[8.5359pt]
{\mu}_{R}(\Delta,x,\theta)\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}x_{S_{1}}+V_{S_{1},0}(x_{S},\beta_{S_{1}})\Delta+\mathcal{L}V_{S_{1},0}(x,\theta)\tfrac{\Delta^{2}}{2}+\mathcal{L}^{2}V_{S_{1},0}(x,\theta)\tfrac{\Delta^{3}}{6}\\[8.5359pt]
x_{S_{2}}+V_{S_{2},0}(x,\beta_{S_{2}})\Delta+\mathcal{L}V_{S_{2},0}(x,\theta)\tfrac{\Delta^{2}}{2}\\[8.5359pt]
x_{R}+V_{R,0}(x,\beta_{R})\Delta\end{bmatrix}.

Notice that the scheme involves
\displaystyle 3d
Gaussian random variables:

\displaystyle\displaystyle B_{j,i+1}-B_{j,i},\quad\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\int_{t_{i}}^{u}dB_{j,v}du,\quad\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\int_{t_{i}}^{u}\int_{t_{i}}^{v}dB_{j,w}dvdu,\qquad 1\leq j\leq d.

The latter of the above integrals appears due to the
application of a third-order stochastic Taylor expansion on
\displaystyle V_{S_{1},0}(x_{S},\beta_{S_{1}})
, in the smoothest component
\displaystyle\bar{X}_{S_{1},i+1}^{\,(\mathrm{II})}
.
As we will show in Section
4
, the log-likelihood based on the local Gaussian scheme (
LG-II
) produces a contrast estimator that is asymptotically unbiased in the high-frequency, complete observation regime. In order for the deduced contrast function to provide desirable asymptotic properties, it is required to include terms up to
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(\Delta_{n}^{3})
in the definition of
\displaystyle\mu_{S_{1}}
, otherwise estimation of the parameter
\displaystyle\beta_{R}
in the model (
Hypo-II
) can be asymptotically biased as
[
32
]
observed for some bivariate hypo-elliptic diffusions in the framework of (
Hypo-I
).

We denote by
\displaystyle\Sigma(\Delta,x,\theta)
the covariance matrix for one-step implementation of scheme
\displaystyle\bar{X}^{(\mathrm{II})}
, with step-size
\displaystyle\Delta>0
, current state
\displaystyle x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
and parameter
\displaystyle\theta\in\Theta
. The covariance matrix is given as:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Sigma(\Delta,x,\theta)=\begin{bmatrix}\Sigma_{S_{1}S_{1}}(\Delta,x,\theta)&\Sigma_{S_{1}S_{2}}(\Delta,x,\theta)&\Sigma_{S_{1}R}(\Delta,x,\theta)\\[5.69046pt]
\Sigma_{S_{2}S_{1}}(\Delta,x,\theta)&\Sigma_{S_{2}S_{2}}(\Delta,x,\theta)&\Sigma_{S_{2}R}(\Delta,x,\theta)\\[5.69046pt]
\Sigma_{RS_{1}}(\Delta,x,\theta)&\Sigma_{RS_{2}}(\Delta,x,\theta)&\Sigma_{RR}(\Delta,x,\theta)\end{bmatrix},

(10)

where each block matrix is specified as: for
\displaystyle x=(x_{S_{1}},x_{S_{2}},x_{R})\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
,
\displaystyle\theta=(\beta_{S_{1}},\beta_{S_{2}},\beta_{R},\sigma)\in\Theta
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\Sigma_{S_{1}S_{1}}(\Delta,x,\theta)\equiv\tfrac{\Delta^{5}}{20}\,a_{S_{1}}(x,\theta),\quad\Sigma_{S_{1}S_{2}}(\Delta,x,\theta)\equiv\tfrac{\Delta^{4}}{8}\,\partial_{x_{S_{2}}}^{\top}V_{S_{1},0}(x_{S},\beta_{S_{1}})\,a_{S_{2}}(x,\theta);\\[8.5359pt]
&\Sigma_{S_{1}R}(\Delta,x,\theta)\equiv\tfrac{\Delta^{3}}{6}\partial_{x_{S_{2}}}^{\top}V_{S_{1},0}(x_{S},\beta_{S_{1}})\partial_{x_{R}}^{\top}V_{S_{2},0}(x,\beta_{S_{2}})a_{R}(x,\sigma),\quad\Sigma_{S_{2}S_{1}}(\Delta,x,\theta)\equiv\Sigma_{S_{1}S_{2}}(\Delta,x,\theta)^{\top};\\[8.5359pt]
&\Sigma_{S_{2}S_{2}}(\Delta,x,\theta)\equiv\tfrac{\Delta^{3}}{3}a_{S_{2}}(x,\theta),\quad\Sigma_{S_{2}R}(\Delta,x,\theta)\equiv\tfrac{\Delta^{2}}{2}\partial_{x_{R}}^{\top}V_{S_{2},0}(x,\beta_{S_{2}})\,a_{R}(x,\sigma),\quad\Sigma_{RS_{1}}(\Delta,x,\theta)\equiv\Sigma_{S_{1}R}(\Delta,x,\theta)^{\top};\\[8.5359pt]
&\Sigma_{RS_{2}}(\Delta,x,\theta)\equiv\Sigma_{S_{2}R}(\Delta,x,\theta)^{\top},\quad\Sigma_{RR}(\Delta,x,\theta)\equiv\Delta\,a_{R}(x,\sigma).\end{aligned}

In the above, we have set

\displaystyle\displaystyle a_{R}(x,\sigma)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{d}V_{R,k}(x,\sigma)V_{R,k}(x,\sigma)^{\top},\quad a_{S_{2}}(x,\theta)=\partial_{x_{R}}^{\top}V_{S_{2},0}(x,\beta_{S_{2}})\,a_{R}(x,\sigma)\,\bigl(\partial_{x_{R}}^{\top}V_{S_{2},0}(x,\beta_{S_{2}})\bigr)^{\top};

\displaystyle\displaystyle a_{S_{1}}(x,\theta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\partial_{x_{S_{2}}}^{\top}V_{S_{1},0}(x_{S},\beta_{S_{1}})\,a_{S_{2}}(x,\theta)\,\bigl(\partial_{x_{S_{2}}}^{\top}V_{S_{1},0}(x_{S},\beta_{S_{1}})\bigr)^{\top}.

Proposition 1
.

Under condition
(H)
-II, it holds that:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\inf_{(x,\sigma)\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\Theta_{\sigma}}|a_{R}(x,\sigma)|>0,\qquad\inf_{(x,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\Theta}|a_{S_{2}}(x,\theta)|>0,\qquad\inf_{(x,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\Theta}|a_{S_{1}}(x,\theta)|>0.

(11)

Then, it also holds that: for any
\displaystyle\Delta>0
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\inf_{(x,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\Theta}|\Sigma(\Delta,x,\theta)|>0.

(12)

The proof is given in Appendix
B
. Due to Proposition
1
, the covariance
\displaystyle\Sigma(\Delta,x,\theta)
is invertible uniformly in
\displaystyle\Delta,x
and
\displaystyle\theta
, thus the approximate log-likelihood based on the local Gaussian discretisation scheme (
LG-II
) is well-defined for the highly degenerate class (
Hypo-II
). We note that, in brief, result (
12
) follows from (
11
) under condition
(H)
-II.

Remark 4
.

For elliptic diffusions,
[
21
,
36
]
assumed

\displaystyle\textstyle\inf_{(x,\sigma)\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\Theta_{\sigma}}|a_{R}(x,\sigma)|>0
, and then established asymptotic properties of developed contrast estimators by introducing additional technical conditions.
For the degenerate diffusion class (
Hypo-II
), we work with condition
(H)
-II that is sufficient for (
11
). We make use of (
11
) in the proof of main results, i.e. when studying the asymptotic properties of the contrast estimator proposed later on.

4
Parameter Inference for Class (
Hypo-II
)

We explore analytically parameter inference procedures for hypo-elliptic diffusions in class (
Hypo-II
). We prove in Section
4.1
that a contrast estimator constructed from the conditionally Gaussian discretisation scheme (
LG-II
) is asymptotically unbiased under the high-frequency, complete observation regime. We illustrate the precise impact of the drift terms involved in
scheme (
LG-II
) on the asymptotic results. In Section
4.2
, we consider the partial observation regime. As observed for the case of class (
Hypo-I
) in the literature, we show via analytical case studies that use of finite-differences for the estimation of hidden paths leads to biased parameter estimates within (
Hypo-II
). Also, we explain that the local Gaussian scheme can be put into effective use within computational approaches for filtering hidden components and estimating parameters.

4.1
Complete Observation Regime

4.1.1
Contrast Estimator

Based on the proposed scheme (
LG-II
) and the corresponding tractable transition density, we construct a contrast estimator for the hypo-elliptic class (
Hypo-II
). We write the transition density of the local Gaussian scheme (
LG-II
), for given
\displaystyle\Delta>0
, current position
\displaystyle x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
and parameter
\displaystyle\theta\in\Theta
as:

\displaystyle\displaystyle y\mapsto\bar{p}_{\Delta}(x,y;\theta)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{(2\pi)^{N}\Delta^{5N_{S_{1}}+3N_{S_{2}}+N_{R}}|\Sigma(x,\theta)|}}\exp\Big(-\tfrac{1}{2}m(\Delta,x,y,\theta)^{\top}\Sigma^{-1}(x,\theta)m(\Delta,x,y,\theta)\Big),

where we have set, for
\displaystyle y=(y_{S_{1}},y_{S_{2}},y_{R})\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{S_{1}}}\times\mathbb{R}^{N_{S_{2}}}\times\mathbb{R}^{N_{R}}
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle m(\Delta,x,y,\theta)=\begin{bmatrix}\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\Delta^{5}}}\bigl(y_{S_{1}}-{\mu}_{S_{1}}(\Delta,x,\theta)\bigr)\\[8.5359pt]
\tfrac{1}{{\sqrt{\Delta^{3}}}}\bigl(y_{S_{2}}-{\mu}_{S_{2}}(\Delta,x,\theta)\bigr)\\[8.5359pt]
\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\Delta}}\bigl(y_{R}-{\mu}_{R}(\Delta,x,\theta)\bigr)\end{bmatrix},\qquad\Sigma(x,\theta)=\Sigma(1,x,\theta).

Note that from Proposition
1
, under Condition
(H)
-II the covariance matrix
\displaystyle\Sigma(x,\theta)
is invertible for any
\displaystyle(x,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\Theta
. We denote by
\displaystyle X^{\,(\mathrm{II})}_{i}
the (complete) observation of a diffusion within class (
Hypo-II
) at time
\displaystyle t_{i}
,
\displaystyle 0\leq i\leq n
. Then, after removing some constant terms from
\displaystyle-2\,\textstyle{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log\bar{p}_{\Delta_{n}}(X_{i-1}^{(\mathrm{II})},X_{i}^{(\mathrm{II})};\theta)}
, we define the following contrast function:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \ell_{n}(\theta)&=\sum_{i=1}^{n}m(\Delta_{n},X_{{i-1}}^{(\mathrm{II})},X_{{i}}^{(\mathrm{II})},\theta)^{\top}\,\Sigma^{-1}(X_{{i-1}}^{(\mathrm{II})},\theta)\,m(\Delta_{n},X_{{i-1}}^{(\mathrm{II})},X_{{i}}^{(\mathrm{II})},\theta)+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log\bigl|\Sigma(X_{{i-1}}^{\,(\mathrm{II})},\theta)\bigr|.\end{aligned}

(13)

Thus, the contrast estimator for the hypo-elliptic class
(
Hypo-II
) is defined as:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{n}=\bigl(\hat{\beta}_{S_{1},n},\,\hat{\beta}_{S_{2},n},\,\hat{\beta}_{R,n},\,\hat{\sigma}_{n}\bigr)=\mathrm{argmin}_{\theta\in\Theta}\;\ell_{n}\bigl(\theta\bigr).

(14)

4.1.2
Asymptotic Results

Before we state our main results, we introduce some conditions for class (
Hypo-II
).

(C1)

\displaystyle V_{j}\in C_{p}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\Theta;\mathbb{R}^{N})
,
\displaystyle 0\leq j\leq d
.

(C2)

For any
\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq N,\,0\leq j\leq d
and
\displaystyle\alpha\in\{1,\ldots,N\}^{l},\,l\geq 0
the following function

\displaystyle\displaystyle\theta\mapsto\partial^{x}_{\alpha}V_{j}^{i}(x,\theta)

is three times differentiable for all
\displaystyle x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
. Furthermore, derivatives of the above map up to the third order are of polynomial growth in
\displaystyle x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
uniformly in
\displaystyle\theta\in\Theta
.

(C3)

The diffusion process
\displaystyle\{X_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
defined via (
Hypo-II
) is ergodic under
\displaystyle\theta=\theta^{\dagger}
, with invariant distribution
\displaystyle\nu_{\theta^{\dagger}}
on
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{N}
. Furthermore, all moments of
\displaystyle\nu_{\theta^{\dagger}}
are finite.

(C4)

It holds that for all
\displaystyle p\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\textstyle\sup_{t>0}\mathbb{E}_{\theta^{\dagger}}[|X_{t}|^{p}]<\infty
.

(C5)

If it holds that

\displaystyle\displaystyle V_{S,0}(x,\beta_{S})=V_{S,0}(x,\beta^{\dagger}_{S}),\ \ V_{R,0}(x,\beta_{R})=V_{R,0}(x,\beta^{\dagger}_{R}),\ \ V_{R}(x,\sigma)=V_{R}(x,\sigma^{\dagger}),

for
\displaystyle x
in set of probability 1 under
\displaystyle\nu_{\theta^{\dagger}}
, then

\displaystyle\beta_{S}=\beta^{\dagger}_{S}
,
\displaystyle\beta_{R}=\beta^{\dagger}_{R}
,
\displaystyle\sigma=\sigma^{\dagger}
.

We write the true value of the parameter for a model in (
Hypo-II
) as

\displaystyle\theta^{\dagger}=(\beta_{S_{1}}^{\dagger},\beta_{S_{2}}^{\dagger},\beta_{R}^{\dagger},\sigma^{\dagger})\in\Theta
.
The latter,
\displaystyle\theta^{\dagger}
, is assumed to lie in the interior of
\displaystyle\Theta
.
Recall the definition of function
\displaystyle V_{0}:\mathbb{R}^{N}\times\Theta\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
in (
3
). Then, the contrast estimator defined in (
14
) has the following asymptotic properties in the high-frequency observation setting.

Theorem 1

(Consistency)
.

Assume that conditions
(H)
-II, (
C1
)–(
C5
) hold. If
\displaystyle n\to\infty
,
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}\to 0
and
\displaystyle n\Delta_{n}\to\infty
, then

\displaystyle\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{n}\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}\theta^{\dagger}.

Theorem 2

(Asymptotic Normality)
.

Assume that conditions
(H)
-II, (
C1
)–(
C5
) hold. If
\displaystyle n\to\infty
,
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}\to 0
and
\displaystyle n\Delta_{n}\to\infty
with
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}=o(n^{-1/2})
, then

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\sqrt{\tfrac{n}{\Delta_{n}^{3}}}\bigl(\hat{\beta}_{S_{1},n}-\beta^{\dagger}_{S_{1}}\bigr)\\[8.5359pt]
\sqrt{\tfrac{n}{\Delta_{n}}}\bigl(\hat{\beta}_{S_{2},n}-\beta^{\dagger}_{S_{2}}\bigr)\\[8.5359pt]
\sqrt{n\Delta_{n}}\bigl(\hat{\beta}_{R,n}-\beta^{\dagger}_{R}\bigr)\\[8.5359pt]
\sqrt{n}\bigl(\hat{\sigma}_{n}-\sigma^{\dagger}\bigr)\end{bmatrix}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{L}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}\mathscr{N}\bigl(\mathbf{0}_{N_{\theta}},\Gamma^{-1}(\theta^{\dagger})\bigr),

where the asymptotic precision matrix
\displaystyle\Gamma(\theta^{\dagger})
is given as:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Gamma(\theta^{\dagger})=\mathrm{diag}\Bigl(\Gamma^{\beta_{S_{1}}}(\theta^{\dagger}),\,\Gamma^{\beta_{S_{2}}}(\theta^{\dagger}),\,\Gamma^{\beta_{R}}(\theta^{\dagger}),\,\Gamma^{\sigma}(\theta^{\dagger})\Bigr),

(15)

with the involved block matrices

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\beta_{S_{1}}}(\theta^{\dagger})\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{\beta_{S_{1}}}\times N_{\beta_{S_{1}}}}
,

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\beta_{S_{2}}}(\theta^{\dagger})\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{\beta_{S_{2}}}\times N_{\beta_{S_{2}}}}
,

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\beta_{R}}(\theta^{\dagger})\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{\beta_{R}}\times N_{\beta_{R}}}
,

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\sigma}(\theta^{\dagger})\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{\sigma}\times N_{\sigma}}

specified as:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Gamma_{ij}^{\beta_{S_{1}}}(\theta^{\dagger})=720\int\partial_{i}^{\beta_{S_{1}}}V_{S_{1},0}(x_{S},\beta^{\dagger}_{S_{1}})^{\top}\,a^{-1}_{S_{1}}(x,\theta^{\dagger})\,\partial_{{j}}^{\beta_{S_{1}}}V_{S_{1},0}(x_{S},\beta^{\dagger}_{S_{1}})\;\nu_{\theta^{\dagger}}(dx);

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Gamma_{ij}^{\beta_{S_{2}}}(\theta^{\dagger})=12\int\partial^{\beta_{S_{2}}}_{i}V_{S_{2},0}(x,\beta^{\dagger}_{S_{2}})^{\top}\,a_{S_{2}}^{-1}(x,\theta^{\dagger})\,\partial^{\beta_{S_{2}}}_{j}V_{S_{2},0}(x,\beta^{\dagger}_{S_{2}})\;\nu_{\theta^{\dagger}}(dx);

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Gamma_{ij}^{\beta_{R}}(\theta^{\dagger})=\int\partial_{{i}}^{\beta_{R}}V_{R,0}(x,\beta^{\dagger}_{R})^{\top}\,a_{R}^{-1}(x,\sigma^{\dagger})\,\partial_{{j}}^{\beta_{R}}V_{R,0}(x,\beta^{\dagger}_{R})\;\nu_{\theta^{\dagger}}(dx);

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Gamma_{ij}^{\sigma}(\theta^{\dagger})=\tfrac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{tr}\bigl(\partial_{i}^{\sigma}\Sigma(x,\theta^{\dagger})\Sigma^{-1}(x,\theta^{\dagger})\partial_{j}^{\sigma}\Sigma(x,\theta^{\dagger})\Sigma^{-1}(x,\theta^{\dagger})\bigr)\;\nu_{\theta^{\dagger}}(dx).

The proofs of Theorems
1
&
2
are given in Appendix
C
.

Remark 5
.

[
14
]

prove consistency and asymptotic normality of a contrast estimator constructed via a locally Gaussian scheme for class (
Hypo-I
).
Our proofs follow a different approach from the one in the above work.
Indicatively, a condition on the step-size of
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}=o(n^{-1/2})
is not required for our proof of consistency, whereas it is needed in
[
14
]
.
We make use of preliminary convergence rates for the estimators
\displaystyle\hat{\beta}_{S_{1},n}
,
\displaystyle\hat{\beta}_{S_{2},n}
and some key identities in the involved matrix calculations to control terms arising in the expansion of the logarithm of the contrast function – this method then provides the consistency for
\displaystyle\hat{\beta}_{R,n}
.
A detailed strategy to show the consistency can be found in Appendix
C.1
. Also, the strategy used in our proofs can be applied to show asymptotic properties of a contrast estimator for a much wider class of highly degenerate diffusions such that condition
(H)
-I or II does not hold, and higher order Lie-brackets, e.g.,
\displaystyle[\widetilde{V}_{0},[\widetilde{V}_{0},[\widetilde{V}_{0},V_{j}]]]
, are required for the uniform Hörmander’s condition. For instance, let
\displaystyle m\geq 3
and consider the following
\displaystyle(m+1)
-dimensional highly degenerate SDE:

\displaystyle\displaystyle dX_{S_{1},t}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=V_{S_{1},0}(X_{S_{2},t},\beta_{S_{1}})dt;

\displaystyle\displaystyle dX_{S_{2},t}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=V_{S_{2},0}(X_{S_{3},t},\beta_{S_{2}})dt;

\displaystyle\displaystyle dX_{S_{m-1},t}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=V_{S_{m-1},0}(X_{S_{m},t},\beta_{S_{m-1}})dt;

\displaystyle\displaystyle dX_{S_{m},t}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=V_{S_{m},0}(X_{R,t},\beta_{S_{m}})dt;

\displaystyle\displaystyle dX_{R,t}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=V_{R,0}(X_{R,t},\beta_{R})dt+V_{R,1}(X_{R,t},\sigma)dB_{1,t},

where
\displaystyle V_{S_{i},0}:\mathbb{R}\times\Theta_{\beta_{S_{i}}}\to\mathbb{R},\,1\leq i\leq m
,
\displaystyle V_{R,0}:\mathbb{R}\times\Theta_{\beta_{R}}\to\mathbb{R}

and
\displaystyle V_{R,1}:\mathbb{R}\times\Theta_{\sigma}\to\mathbb{R}
with some compact parameter spaces
\displaystyle\Theta_{\beta_{S_{i}}}
,
\displaystyle\Theta_{\beta_{R}}
and
\displaystyle\Theta_{\sigma}
. We assume a similar condition to
(H)
so that the uniform Hörmander’s condition (
5
) holds with
\displaystyle M=m
. We provide a recipe to construct an asymptotically unbiased contrast estimator for such a general class of degenerate diffusions in Section
6
. In brief, given the deduced contrast estimator, the proof of consistency will start by showing the consistency of
\displaystyle\hat{\beta}_{S_{1},n}
and obtaining a convergence rate. Then, utilising this result, we would next prove the consistency of
\displaystyle\hat{\beta}_{S_{2},n}
and get its convergence rate. We would repeat this procedure until we show the consistency of the estimators for
\displaystyle(\beta_{R},\sigma)
.

4.1.3
Case Study – Bias due to Incorrect Drift Expansion

We have proven that the contrast estimator based on the proposed scheme (
LG-II
) is asymptotically unbiased under the high-frequency, complete observation regime. The inclusion of an appropriate number terms from the stochastic Taylor expansion of
\displaystyle V_{S_{1},0}(X_{S_{1},t},X_{S_{2},t},\beta_{S_{1}})
and
\displaystyle V_{S_{2},0}(X_{t},\beta_{S_{2}})
in scheme (
LG-II
) is critical for obtaining desirable asymptotic properties.
Omission of such terms will typically give rise to an asymptotic bias.
In this subsection, we briefly highlight the effect of the ‘drift correction’ via a simple three-dimensional hypo-elliptic model from (
Hypo-II
).
We consider the following SDE:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{aligned} dq_{t}&=p_{t}dt;\\
dp_{t}&=s_{t}dt;\\
ds_{t}&=-\beta s_{t}dt+\sigma dB_{t},\end{aligned}

(16)

where
\displaystyle\theta=(\beta,\sigma)
is the parameter vector. We assume that all components of the system are observed, and consider the following discretisation scheme for SDE (
16
):

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \bar{x}_{i+1}=\begin{bmatrix}\bar{q}_{i+1}\\[5.69046pt]
\bar{p}_{i+1}\\[5.69046pt]
\bar{s}_{i+1}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\bar{q}_{i}+\bar{p}_{i}\Delta_{n}+\bar{s}_{i}\tfrac{\Delta_{n}^{2}}{2}\\[8.5359pt]
\bar{p}_{i}+\bar{s}_{i}\Delta_{n}-\beta\bar{s}_{i}\tfrac{\Delta^{2}_{n}}{2}\\[8.5359pt]
\bar{s}_{i}-\beta\bar{s}_{i}\Delta_{n}\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}\sigma\times\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\int_{t_{i}}^{u}\int_{t_{i}}^{v}dB_{w}dvdu\\[8.5359pt]
\sigma\times\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\int_{t_{i}}^{u}dB_{v}du\\[8.5359pt]
\sigma\times(B_{t_{i+1}}-B_{t_{i}})\end{bmatrix}.\end{aligned}

(17)

Thus, terms of size
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(\Delta_{n}^{3})
are not included in the deterministic part of the approximation
\displaystyle\bar{q}_{i+1}
of the smoothest component.
Based on the conditionally Gaussian scheme (
17
), we define a contrast function as
\displaystyle(-2)\times
(complete log-likelihood), that is, after some constants are removed:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\ell_{n}(\theta;x_{0:n})=6n\log\sigma+\tfrac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}m(\Delta_{n},x_{i-1},x_{i},\theta)^{\top}\,\Sigma^{-1}\,m(\Delta_{n},x_{i-1},x_{i},\theta),

where we have set
\displaystyle x_{i}=[q_{i},p_{i},s_{i}]^{\top}
,
\displaystyle 0\leq i\leq n
, and

\displaystyle\displaystyle m(\Delta_{n},x_{i-1},x_{i},\theta)=\begin{bmatrix}\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\Delta_{n}^{5}}}\bigl(q_{i}-q_{i-1}-p_{i-1}\Delta_{n}-s_{i-1}\tfrac{\Delta_{n}^{2}}{2}\bigr)\\[8.5359pt]
\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\Delta_{n}^{3}}}\bigl(p_{i}-p_{i-1}-s_{i-1}\Delta_{n}+\beta s_{i-1}\tfrac{\Delta^{2}_{n}}{2}\bigr)\\[8.5359pt]
\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\Delta_{n}}}\bigl(s_{i}-s_{i-1}+\beta s_{i-1}\Delta_{n}\bigr)\end{bmatrix},\quad{\Sigma}=\begin{bmatrix}\tfrac{1}{20}&\tfrac{1}{8}&\tfrac{1}{6}\\[5.69046pt]
\tfrac{1}{8}&\tfrac{1}{3}&\tfrac{1}{2}\\[8.5359pt]
\tfrac{1}{6}&\tfrac{1}{2}&1\end{bmatrix}.

Solving
\displaystyle\partial_{\beta}\,\ell_{n}(\theta;x_{0:n})=0
, we obtain the contrast estimator for
\displaystyle\beta
as
\displaystyle\widetilde{\beta}_{n}={\widetilde{g}_{n}}/{\widetilde{f}_{n}}
, where we have defined:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\widetilde{f}_{n}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\Bigl\{\tfrac{1}{2}\Sigma^{-1}_{32}+\Sigma^{-1}_{33}+\tfrac{1}{4}\Sigma^{-1}_{22}+\tfrac{1}{2}\Sigma^{-1}_{23}\Bigr\}\times\tfrac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}s_{i-1}^{2};

\displaystyle\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{n}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=-\tfrac{1}{n\sqrt{\Delta_{n}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}s_{i-1}\times\Bigl\{\Sigma^{-1}_{31}\times\tfrac{q_{i}-q_{i-1}-p_{i-1}\Delta_{n}-s_{i-1}\tfrac{\Delta_{n}^{2}}{2}}{\sqrt{\Delta_{n}^{5}}}\;+\;\Sigma^{-1}_{32}\times\tfrac{p_{i}-p_{i-1}-s_{i-1}\Delta_{n}}{\sqrt{\Delta_{n}^{3}}}+\Sigma^{-1}_{33}\times\tfrac{s_{i}-s_{i-1}}{\sqrt{\Delta_{n}}}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\quad+\tfrac{1}{2}\Sigma^{-1}_{21}\times\tfrac{q_{i}-q_{i-1}-p_{i-1}\Delta_{n}-s_{i-1}\tfrac{\Delta_{n}^{2}}{2}}{\sqrt{\Delta_{n}^{5}}}+\tfrac{1}{2}\Sigma^{-1}_{22}\times\tfrac{p_{i}-p_{i-1}-s_{i-1}\Delta_{n}}{\sqrt{\Delta_{n}^{3}}}+\tfrac{1}{2}\Sigma^{-1}_{23}\times\tfrac{s_{i}-s_{i-1}}{\sqrt{\Delta_{n}}}\Bigr\}.

From the ergodicity of the process
\displaystyle\{s_{t}\}
and Lemma
2
in the Appendix, we have that as
\displaystyle n\to\infty
,
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}\to 0
and
\displaystyle n\Delta_{n}\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\widetilde{f}_{n}\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}c_{1}\times\int s^{2}\,\nu_{\theta^{\dagger}}(ds),

for a non-zero constant
\displaystyle c_{1}={\Sigma}_{32}^{-1}/2+{\Sigma}_{33}^{-1}+{\Sigma}_{22}^{-1}/4+{\Sigma}_{23}^{-1}/2
, where
\displaystyle\nu_{\theta}^{\dagger}(ds)
is the invariant distribution of
\displaystyle\{s_{t}\}
under the true parameter
\displaystyle\theta^{\dagger}
.
For the numerator
\displaystyle\widetilde{g_{n}}
, we apply Lemmas
2
&
3
in Appendix to obtain that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\widetilde{g}_{n}\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}(c_{1}+c_{2})\times\beta^{\dagger}\times\int s^{2}\,\nu_{\theta^{\dagger}}(ds),

for a non-zero constant
\displaystyle c_{2}\equiv{\Sigma}_{31}^{-1}/6+{\Sigma}_{21}^{-1}/12
. Hence, it holds that, if
\displaystyle n\to\infty
,
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}\to 0
and
\displaystyle n\Delta_{n}\to\infty
, then

\displaystyle\displaystyle\widetilde{\beta}_{n}\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}\bigl(1+\tfrac{c_{2}}{c_{1}}\bigr)\times\beta^{\dagger}.

Thus, the drift estimation based on the discretisation scheme (
17
) with inappropriate drift expansion is, in general, asymptotically biased. One can check that the above bias is removed upon use of our locally Gaussian scheme (
LG-II
) instead of (
17
).

4.2
Partial Observation Regime

4.2.1
Case Study – Bias due to Finite-Differences

To motivate the ‘appropriateness’ of the proposed locally Gaussian scheme (
LG-II
) in the context of parameter inference in a partial observation setting, we illustrate that naive use of finite-differences to impute hidden components (a quite common in applications) induces a bias in the estimation of the SDE parameters. To observe this, consider the model (
16
) again but with the drift parameter
\displaystyle\beta
fixed to 1:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{aligned} dq_{t}&=p_{t}dt;\\
dp_{t}&=s_{t}dt;\\
ds_{t}&=-s_{t}dt+\sigma dB_{t},\end{aligned}

(18)

for
\displaystyle\sigma>0
. We then apply the Euler-Maruyama scheme for the first equation of (
18
) and the locally Gaussian scheme (
LG-I
) for the remaining dynamics, i.e.,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \begin{bmatrix}\bar{q}_{i+1}\\[2.84544pt]
\bar{p}_{i+1}\\[2.84544pt]
\bar{s}_{i+1}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\bar{q}_{i}+\bar{p}_{i}\Delta_{n}\\[2.84544pt]
\bar{p}_{i}+\bar{s}_{i}\Delta_{n}-\bar{s}_{i}\tfrac{\Delta_{n}^{2}}{2}\\[2.84544pt]
\bar{s}_{i}-\bar{s}_{i}\Delta_{n}\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}0\\[8.5359pt]
\sigma\times\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\int_{t_{i}}^{u}dB_{v}du\\[8.5359pt]
\sigma\times\bigl(B_{i+1}-B_{i}\bigr)\end{bmatrix}.\end{aligned}

(19)

Scheme (
19
) is degenerate since the upper-most equation does not involve noise.
We now consider the estimator based on the likelihood provided by (
19
), given the discrete-time observations
\displaystyle\{q_{0:n},s_{0:n}\}
, and with the hidden paths
\displaystyle p_{0:n}
imputed via the first equation of (
19
) using the observations
\displaystyle q_{0:n}
.

Remark 6
.

In practice, the rough component
\displaystyle s_{t}
is often not observed, so one must impute the missing components
\displaystyle s_{0:n}
conditionally on the observations
\displaystyle q_{0:n}
by making use of the transition density (or some approximation of it) for both coordinates
\displaystyle(p,s)
of (
18
).
One would reasonably expect that presence of bias will be typical in such a practical scenario, if it is found to be present in the simpler case when
\displaystyle s_{t}
is directly observed.

The complete likelihood of the discretisation scheme (
19
) is given as:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Biggl\{\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{(2\pi)^{2}\Delta_{n}^{4}\sigma^{4}|\Sigma|}}\exp\Bigl(-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\,m(\Delta_{n},y_{i-1},y_{i})^{\top}\,\Sigma^{-1}\,m(\Delta_{n},y_{i-1},y_{i})\Bigr)\times\delta\bigl(q_{i}-q_{i-1}-p_{i-1}\Delta_{n}\bigr)\Biggr\},\end{aligned}

where we have defined
\displaystyle y_{i}=[p_{i},s_{i}]^{\top}
,
\displaystyle 0\leq i\leq n
, and

\displaystyle\displaystyle m(\Delta_{n},y_{i-1},y_{i})=\begin{bmatrix}\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\Delta_{n}^{3}}}\bigl(p_{i}-p_{i-1}-s_{i-1}\Delta_{n}+s_{i-1}\tfrac{\Delta_{n}^{2}}{2}\bigr)\\[5.69046pt]
\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{\Delta_{n}}}\bigl(s_{i}-s_{i-1}+s_{i-1}\Delta_{n}\bigr)\end{bmatrix},\quad\Sigma=\begin{bmatrix}\tfrac{1}{3}&\tfrac{1}{2}\\[5.69046pt]
\tfrac{1}{2}&1\end{bmatrix}.

Integrating out
\displaystyle p_{0:n}
, we obtain the marginal likelihood
\displaystyle f_{n}(\sigma\,;q_{0:n+1},s_{0:n})
as:

\displaystyle\displaystyle f_{n}(\sigma\,;q_{0:n+1},s_{0:n})=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Biggl\{\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{(2\pi)^{2}\Delta_{n}^{4}\sigma^{4}|\Sigma|}}\exp\Bigl(-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\,m(\Delta_{n},\hat{y}_{i-1},\hat{y}_{i})^{\top}\,\Sigma^{-1}\,m(\Delta_{n},\hat{y}_{i-1},\hat{y}_{i})\Bigr)\Biggr\},

where
\displaystyle\hat{y}_{i}=[\hat{p_{i}},s_{i}]^{\top}
, with
\displaystyle\hat{p}_{i}=\bigl(q_{i+1}-q_{i}\bigr)/\Delta_{n}
.
Then, we obtain the following contrast function for
\displaystyle\sigma
, after removing constant terms from
\displaystyle(-2)\times\log f_{n}(\sigma;q_{0:n+1},s_{0:n})
:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\ell_{n}(\theta;q_{0:n+1},s_{0:n})=4n\log\sigma+\tfrac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}m(\Delta_{n},\hat{y}_{i-1},\hat{y}_{i})^{\top}\,{\Sigma}^{-1}\,m(\Delta_{n},\hat{y}_{i-1},\hat{y}_{i}).

(20)

Solving
\displaystyle\partial_{\sigma}\ell_{n}(\sigma;q_{0:n+1},s_{0:n})=0
, we obtain the estimator
\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{n}
, such that:

\displaystyle\displaystyle(\hat{\sigma}_{n})^{2}=\tfrac{1}{2n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}m(\Delta_{n},\hat{y}_{i-1},\hat{y}_{i})^{\top}\,{\Sigma}^{-1}\,m(\Delta_{n},\hat{y}_{i-1},\hat{y}_{i}).

It holds that, if
\displaystyle n\to\infty
,
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}\to 0
and
\displaystyle n\Delta_{n}\to\infty
, then

\displaystyle\displaystyle(\hat{\sigma}_{n})^{2}\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\dagger}}}\tfrac{8}{5}(\sigma^{\dagger})^{2}.

(21)

We prove convergence (
21
) in Appendix
E
. The proof indicates that the bias arises from the higher order stochastic Taylor expansion terms of
\displaystyle q_{i+1}
which are ignored by the estimate
\displaystyle\hat{p}_{i}
. Thus, use of the finite-difference approximation
for imputation of component
\displaystyle p_{t}
,
induces an asymptotic bias at the estimation of
\displaystyle\sigma
.

4.2.2
Filtering and Parameter Inference via the Proposed Scheme (
LG-II
)

We put forward the locally Gaussian scheme (
LG-II
) for imputing hidden components and performing parameter inference under a partial observation regime. The scheme and its transition density on the full set of coordinates can be combined with various computational methods, e.g., Monte-Carlo Expectation Maximisation (MC-EM) and Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC), similarly to earlier works
[
8
,
32
]
that applied some conditionally Gaussian schemes for inference of specific hypo-elliptic models within the class (
Hypo-I
).

We now highlight the use of a relatively straightforward Kalman filter recursion for carrying out statistical inference once the locally Gaussian scheme is adopted,
for a rich sub-class of hypo-elliptic models, referred to here as
conditionally Gaussian non-linear systems
.
That is, the system is originally specified as a non-linear SDE but can be treated as a linear system given components that correspond to observations. For elliptic diffusions with such a structure, continuous-time filtering and smoothing have been investigated in engineering, see e.g. Chapter 8 of
[
6
]
. Several important hypo-elliptic models used in applications fall within this sub-class, e.g., standard Langevin equations, Quasi-Markovian generalised Langevin Equations (
QGLE-I
,
QGLE-II
). Here, our interest lies in the sub-class derived via the general model (
Hypo-II
) once the constituent coefficients are specified as:

\displaystyle\displaystyle V_{S_{1},0}(x_{S},\beta_{S_{1}})=C_{\beta_{S_{1}}}x_{S_{1}}+\hat{C}_{\beta_{S_{1}}}x_{S_{2}},\quad V_{S_{2},0}(x,\beta_{S_{2}})=f_{S_{2}}(x_{S_{1}},\beta_{S_{1}})+C_{\beta_{S_{2}}}x_{H};

\displaystyle\displaystyle V_{R,0}(x,\beta_{R})=f_{R}(x_{S_{1}},\beta_{R})+C_{\beta_{R}}x_{H},\quad V_{R,j}(x,\sigma)=f_{R,j}(\sigma),\quad 1\leq j\leq d,

for
\displaystyle x=(x_{S_{1}},x_{S_{2}},x_{R})=(x_{S_{1}},x_{H})\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{S_{1}}}\times\mathbb{R}^{N_{S_{2}}+N_{R}}
and
\displaystyle\theta=(\beta_{S_{1}},\beta_{S_{2}},\beta_{R},\sigma)\in\Theta
, where: (i)
\displaystyle f_{S_{2}}
,
\displaystyle f_{R}
,
\displaystyle f_{R,j}
are vector-valued functions, allowed to be non-linear w.r.t. the state
\displaystyle x_{S_{1}}
; (ii) matrices

\displaystyle\displaystyle{C}_{\beta_{S_{1}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{S_{1}}\times N_{S_{1}}},\qquad\hat{C}_{\beta_{S_{1}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{S_{1}}\times N_{S_{2}}},\qquad C_{\beta_{S_{2}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{S_{2}}\times(N_{S_{2}}+N_{R})},\qquad C_{\beta_{R}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{R}\times(N_{S_{2}}+N_{R})}

are independent of the state
\displaystyle x
. Critically, given the observable component
\displaystyle x_{S_{1}}
, the drift functions are linear functions of the hidden component
\displaystyle x_{H}
. For the model with the above choice of coefficients, the locally Gaussian scheme (
LG-II
) writes as:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bar{X}_{i+1}=\begin{bmatrix}\bar{X}_{S_{1},i+1}\\[5.69046pt]
\bar{X}_{S_{2},i+1}\\[5.69046pt]
\bar{X}_{R,i+1}\end{bmatrix}=b(\Delta_{n},\bar{X}_{S_{1},i},\theta)+A(\Delta_{n},\bar{X}_{S_{1},i},\theta)\begin{bmatrix}\bar{X}_{S_{2},i}\\[2.84544pt]
\bar{X}_{R,i}\end{bmatrix}+w_{i}(\Delta_{n},\theta),

(22)

for functions

\displaystyle b:(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{N_{S_{1}}}\times\Theta\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
,

\displaystyle A:(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{N_{S_{1}}}\times\Theta\to\mathbb{R}^{N\times(N_{S_{2}}+N_{R})}
and an
\displaystyle N
-dimensional Gaussian variate
\displaystyle w_{i}(\Delta_{n},\theta)
. Since the
right-hand side of scheme (
22
) is linear w.r.t. the hidden components
\displaystyle\bar{X}_{S_{2},i},\,\bar{X}_{R,i}
given the observed component
\displaystyle\bar{X}_{S_{1},i}
, one can obtain Kalman filtering and smoothing recursions, and calculate the marginal likelihood for the observations
\displaystyle\bar{X}_{S_{1},0:n}
. We provide the closed form filtering and marginal likelihood calculations in Appendix
F
. We use these tools in the numerical experiments of parameter inference under the partial observation regime in Section
5
that follows.

Remark 7
.

[
37
]
studied parameter inference for the QGLE of first-type in (
QGLE-I
), where they applied an Euler-Maruyama scheme to construct Kalman filtering and smoothing for the rough components
\displaystyle(p_{t},s_{t})
given the velocity
\displaystyle p_{t}
, with values of the latter obtained (via finite-differences) from discrete observations of the position
\displaystyle q_{t}
. Then, they used Kalman filtering and smoothing within an Expectation-Maximisation (EM) algorithm to estimate the parameters. However, as we have seen, such a finite-differences approach can induce bias in the estimation of the diffusion parameters.

5
Numerical Studies

5.1
Linear SDE in a Partial Observation Regime

We illustrate empirically, for an example SDE model, that parameter estimation via the proposed locally Gaussian scheme (
LG-II
) leads to asymptotically unbiased estimation under the partial observation regime. We also highlight the effect of the drift correction in the properties of the estimators. We again consider the model studied in Section
4.1.3
, that is,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{aligned} dq_{t}&=p_{t}dt;\\
dp_{t}&=s_{t}dt;\\
ds_{t}&=-\beta s_{t}dt+\sigma dB_{t},\end{aligned}

where
\displaystyle\theta=(\beta,\sigma)\in\Theta=(0,\infty)\times(0,\infty)
is the parameter vector. In agreement with practice, we assume that only discrete observations of the smoothest component,
\displaystyle q_{0:n}
, are available, with an equidistant step-size
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}
. We compute the following two estimators based on two different discretisation schemes:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{n,j}=(\hat{\beta}_{n,j},\hat{\sigma}_{n,j})={\rm argmax}_{\theta\in\Theta}\log p_{j}(\theta;q_{0:n}),\quad j=1,2,

where
\displaystyle p_{1}(\theta;q_{0:n})
is the approximate likelihood of the observations as obtained by use of Kalman filter in the setting of our locally Gaussian scheme (
LG-II
):

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \begin{bmatrix}\bar{q}_{i+1}\\[2.84544pt]
\bar{p}_{i+1}\\[2.84544pt]
\bar{s}_{i+1}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\bar{q}_{i}+\bar{p}_{i}\Delta_{n}+\bar{s}_{i}\tfrac{\Delta_{n}^{2}}{2}-\beta\bar{s}_{i}\tfrac{\Delta_{n}^{3}}{6}\\[8.5359pt]
\bar{p}_{i}+\bar{s}_{i}\Delta_{n}-\beta\bar{s}_{i}\tfrac{\Delta_{n}^{2}}{2}\\[8.5359pt]
\bar{s}_{i}-\beta\bar{s}_{i}\Delta_{n}\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}\sigma\times\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\int_{t_{i}}^{u}\int_{t_{i}}^{v}dB_{w}dvdu\\[8.5359pt]
\sigma\times\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\int_{t_{i}}^{v}dB_{v}du\\[8.5359pt]
\sigma\times\bigl(B_{i+1}-B_{i}\bigr)\end{bmatrix},\end{aligned}

and
\displaystyle p_{2}(\theta;q_{0:n})
is a different approximate likelihood obtained in the setting of the following conditionally Gaussian scheme that omits higher-order correction terms of order
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(\Delta_{n}^{2})
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(\Delta_{n}^{3})
from the stochastic Taylor expansion of the drift function of component
\displaystyle p
and
\displaystyle q
:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \begin{bmatrix}\widetilde{q}_{i+1}\\[2.84544pt]
\widetilde{p}_{i+1}\\[2.84544pt]
\widetilde{s}_{i+1}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\widetilde{q}_{i}+\widetilde{p}_{i}\Delta_{n}\\[8.5359pt]
\widetilde{p}_{i}+\widetilde{s}_{i}\Delta_{n}\\[8.5359pt]
\widetilde{s}_{i}-\beta\widetilde{s}_{i}\Delta_{n}\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}\sigma\times\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\int_{t_{i}}^{u}\int_{t_{i}}^{v}dB_{w}dvdu\\[8.5359pt]
\sigma\times\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\int_{t_{i}}^{v}dB_{v}du\\[8.5359pt]
\sigma\times\bigl(B_{i+1}-B_{i}\bigr)\end{bmatrix}.\end{aligned}

(23)

We generate
\displaystyle 50
independent realisations of the dataset
\displaystyle q_{0:n}
by sub-sampling trajectories obtained from scheme (
LG-II
) with a small step-size
\displaystyle 10^{-4}
. We have chosen the scheme because it is expected to have a better accuracy than other classical schemes (such as Euler-Maruyama scheme) due to the higher order stochastic Taylor expansion of drift functions. We consider the following three high-frequency scenarios for the data:

Set I
.
\displaystyle n=5\cdot 10^{5},\,\Delta_{n}=10^{-3},\,T(=n\Delta_{n})=500
.

Set II
.
\displaystyle n=10^{6},\,\Delta_{n}=5\cdot 10^{-4},\,{T=500}
.

Set III
.
\displaystyle n=10^{7},\,\Delta_{n}=10^{-3},\,T=10^{4}
.

(a)

Set I.

(b)

Set II.

(c)

Set III.

Figure 1
:
Parameter estimates from
\displaystyle{50}
independent realisations of partial observations: Each blue
\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\circ}
and red
\displaystyle\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\times}
in the three figures represents one realisation of
\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{n,1}
(using (
LG-II
)) and
\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{n,2}
(using incorrect discretisation (
23
)), respectively. The true value is
\displaystyle\theta^{\dagger}=(\beta^{\dagger},\sigma^{\dagger})=(2.0,4.0)
.

The true parameter value is set to
\displaystyle\theta^{\dagger}=(\beta^{\dagger},\sigma^{\dagger})=(2.0,4.0)
, and the Nelder-Mead method is applied to optimise the marginal likelihoods with the initial guess set to
\displaystyle(\beta_{0},\sigma_{0})=(3.0,3.0)
.
In Figure
1
, we plot the
\displaystyle{50}
realisations of the two different estimators. Table
3
summarizes the mean and standard deviation of relative error of estimate, i.e.,
\displaystyle(\hat{\theta}_{n,j}-\theta^{\dagger})/\theta^{\dagger},\,j=1,2
, from the
\displaystyle{50}
repetitions.
First, we observe that the estimates of
\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{n,1}
(using our scheme (
LG-II
)) are centred at the true value in all scenarios, thus in this case we have an empirical illustration of an asymptotically unbiased estimation in the partial observation setting. Secondly, it is clear from the figures and the table that the mean of estimates of
\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{n,2}
(estimator based on the conditionally Gaussian scheme without appropriate drift correction (
23
)) is shifted from the true value, and seems to be centred at
\displaystyle(\beta,\sigma)=(2.10,3.960)
. Thus
\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{n,2}
induces an asymptotic bias in the partial observation regime, in agreement with the case study in Section
4.1.3
in the complete observation case.
Notably, there is a clear separation between the two estimators of
\displaystyle\sigma
, and the range of realisations of
\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{n,2}
does not cover the true value
\displaystyle\sigma^{\dagger}=4.0
for all scenarios. Comparing Set I and III in Figure
1
, we observe that the variance of drift parameter estimation decreases as
\displaystyle T
increases, and then the bias in
\displaystyle\hat{\beta}_{n,2}
becomes more clear in Set III. We stress here that the bias in
\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{n,2}
is not removed with increasing
\displaystyle n
or decreasing
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}
. Also, one can still observe the bias even if the datasets are obtained with other numerical schemes, e.g., Euler-Maruyama scheme, rather than scheme (
LG-II
).

5.2
Quasi-Markovian Generalised Langevin Equations

5.2.1
Scalar Extended State

We consider the QGLE describing one-dimensional positional domain:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{aligned} dq_{t}&=p_{t}dt;\\[2.84544pt]
dp_{t}&=\bigl(-{U^{\prime}}(q_{t})+\lambda s_{t}\bigr)dt;\\[2.84544pt]
ds_{t}&=(-\lambda p_{t}-\alpha s_{t})dt+\sigma dB_{t},\quad(q_{0},p_{0},s_{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{3},\end{aligned}

(24)

where
\displaystyle\alpha>0,\,\sigma>0,\,\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
and
\displaystyle U:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
. In this experiment, we consider the following two choices of potential
\displaystyle U
:

\displaystyle\displaystyle q\mapsto U_{\mathrm{HO}}(q)=D\times\tfrac{q^{2}}{2},\qquad q\mapsto U_{\mathrm{DW}}(q)=D\times\tfrac{q^{2}}{2}+\sin\Bigl(\tfrac{1}{4}+2q\Bigr),

where
\displaystyle D>0
is a parameter. The function
\displaystyle U_{\mathrm{DW}}
(used in experiments in the work
[
24
]
) represents an uneven double well potential, under which model (
24
) is non-linear. We generate 50 independent datasets by sub-sampling trajectories produced by the discretisation scheme (
LG-II
) with a small step-size
\displaystyle 10^{-4}
so that obtained observations correspond to
\displaystyle n=2\times 10^{5},\,\Delta_{n}=10^{-3},\,T=n\Delta_{n}=200
. For the complete observation regime, we compute the contrast estimator (
14
) for each given dataset. For experiments of partial observations, we use the trajectories of the position
\displaystyle q_{t}
only, extracted from the complete observations, and compute the MLE by maximising the marginal likelihood obtained from the Kalman recursion formula under the locally Gaussian scheme (
LG-II
), as shown in Section
4.2.2
. To minimise
the relevant target functions
we use the adaptive moments (Adam) optimiser with the following algorithmic specifications: (step-size) =
\displaystyle 0.2
, (exponential decay rate for the first moment estimates) =
\displaystyle 0.9
,
(exponential decay rate for the first moment estimates) =
\displaystyle 0.9
, (exponential decay rate for the second moment estimates) =
\displaystyle 0.999
, (additive term for numerical stability) =
\displaystyle 10^{-8}
and (number of iteration) =
\displaystyle 2,000
. The true parameters are set to
\displaystyle\theta^{\dagger}=(D^{\dagger},\lambda^{\dagger},\alpha^{\dagger},\sigma^{\dagger})=(1.0,2.0,4.0,4.0)
.
Also, the initial guesses for the parameter are set to
\displaystyle(D_{0},\lambda_{0},\alpha_{0},\sigma_{0})=(3.0,3.0,3.0,3.0)
.
We summarise the mean and standard deviation of
\displaystyle(\mathrm{MLE}-\theta^{\dagger})/\theta^{\dagger}
from the 50 independent trajectories in Table
4
. We notice that the results for the complete observation regime are in agreement with the analytical results in Theorem
2
. For instance, convergence to the true values appears to be faster for parameters
\displaystyle(D,\lambda)
in the smooth component
\displaystyle p_{t}
(recall the convergence rate
\displaystyle\sqrt{\Delta_{n}/n}
for such parameters in the CLT of Theorem
2
). Besides, under the partial observation regime, the estimates seem to be centred around the true parameter as well, with standard deviations that are larger than the ones in the case of complete observations (as expected). Thus, parameter inference carried out via the proposed locally Gaussian scheme (
LG-II
) appears in this case to provide unbiased estimates in the partial observation regime.

).

):
The free energy used in the experiment.
Right panel (
2(b)
): A trajectory of the observable coordinate
\displaystyle q_{t}
from the QGLE (
25
).

5.2.2
Multivariate Extended State

We consider a QGLE with one-dimensional coordinates and multivariate extended variable, motivated from the work of
[
1
]
that studies protein-folding kinetics via a Quasi-Markovian GLE (
QGLE-II
) and showcases that a QGLE accurately reproduces simulations of molecular dynamics (MD) that involve memory effects in the friction. In their investigation, a one-dimensional reaction coordinate,
\displaystyle q_{t}
, given as the sum of the separations between native contacts, is modelled via the following QGLE:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{aligned} dq_{t}&=\tfrac{1}{m}\times p_{t}\,dt;\\[2.84544pt]
dp_{t}&=-{U^{\prime}}(q_{t})dt+\sum_{l=1}^{L}s_{l,t}dt;\\[2.84544pt]
ds_{l,t}&=-\tfrac{1}{\tau_{l}}\times s_{l,t}\,dt-\tfrac{c_{l}}{\tau_{l}}\times p_{t}\,dt+\tfrac{\sqrt{2\beta^{-1}c_{l}}}{\tau_{l}}\,dB_{l,t},\quad s_{l,0}\sim\mathscr{N}(0,\beta^{-1}),\quad\,1\leq l\leq L,\end{aligned}

(25)

where
\displaystyle m,\beta>0
denote the mass and the inverse thermal energy respectively,
\displaystyle\{c_{l},\tau_{l}\}_{1\leq l\leq L}
are the unknown parameters taking positive values, for
\displaystyle L\geq 1
, and
\displaystyle U:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, the folding free energy landscape for proteins, is specified as
\displaystyle q\mapsto U(q)=-\beta^{-1}\log\nu(q)
with
\displaystyle\nu(\cdot)
being the equilibrium probability density function. QGLE (
25
) corresponds to the non-Markovian GLE (
GLE
) with the memory kernel given as a so-called
Prony series
:

\displaystyle\displaystyle K(t)=\sum_{l=1}^{L}\frac{c_{l}}{\tau_{l}}\times\exp\Bigl(-\frac{t}{\tau_{l}}\Bigr),\quad t\geq 0.

(26)

[
1
]
constructed QGLE (
25
) with
\displaystyle L=5
by determining the parameters via a least squares method so that the memory kernel (
26
) fits the one extracted numerically from the observed time-series of
\displaystyle q
.

In our experiment, we estimate the unknown parameters by maximising the marginal likelihood given the partial observations
\displaystyle q_{0:n}
.
Since the mass (
\displaystyle m
) of a particle can typically be measured, we assume
\displaystyle m
to be known and set
\displaystyle m=1
, in agreement with numerical experiments in the literature
[
8
,
32
]
. We set
\displaystyle L=2
, and specify the free energy function as
\displaystyle q\mapsto U(q)=a(q-q_{\mathrm{min}})^{2}(q-q_{\mathrm{max}})^{2}+bq^{3}
with constants
\displaystyle(q_{\mathrm{min}},q_{\mathrm{max}},a,b)=(0.30,0.90,1200,0.001)
. We set
\displaystyle\beta^{-1}=2.949
and select the true parameters as
\displaystyle\theta^{\dagger}=(c_{1}^{\dagger},\tau_{1}^{\dagger},c_{2}^{\dagger},\tau_{2}^{\dagger})=(0.22,0.007,1.2,4.6)
, as such a choice closely reproduces the shape of the memory kernel estimated in
[
1
]
. We generate
\displaystyle 50
independent trajectories of
\displaystyle q
on the time inverval
\displaystyle[0,1500]
by applying scheme (
LG-II
) to QGLE (
25
) with step-size
\displaystyle 10^{-4}
. We discard the observations up to time
\displaystyle 500
and sub-sample the datasets
\displaystyle q_{0:n}
in equilibrium, so that
\displaystyle n=10^{6}
,
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}=10^{-3}
,
\displaystyle T=1000
.
In Figure
2
, we plot the shape of the free energy
\displaystyle U
and one trajectory of the component
\displaystyle q
from the QGLE (
25
) in the chosen setting. Notice that QGLE (
25
) is a conditionally Gaussian non-linear system (given the component
\displaystyle q
), thus
upon adoption of the locally Gaussian discretisation (
LG-II
),
the marginal likelihood can be calculated via the Kalman filter shown in Section
4.2.2
. We use the Nelder-Mead method to optimise the marginal likelihoods with the initial value
\displaystyle\theta_{0}=(0.1,0.01,1.0,10.0)
.
Table
5
summarises the mean and standard deviation of relative error of estimates

\displaystyle\hat{\theta_{n}}=(\hat{c}_{1,n},\hat{\tau}_{1,n},\hat{c}_{2,n},\hat{\tau}_{2,n})
. The estimation results are overall acceptable though the estimator
\displaystyle\hat{\tau}_{1,n}
is slightly biased and centred around
\displaystyle 0.0073
– we have found empirically that such bias disappears upon chosing a smaller step-size
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}
, e.g.
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}=2\times 10^{-4}
, so that
\displaystyle n\Delta_{n}^{2}
becomes closer to
\displaystyle 0
.

We typically observe that the standard derviation of estimates for
\displaystyle(c_{1},\tau_{1})
is much smaller than that for
\displaystyle(c_{2},\tau_{2})
. In Figure
3
, we plot the memory kernel (
26
) with the parameters set equal to the mean value of the
\displaystyle 50
MLEs to observe the level of agreement between the estimated memory kernel and the reference kernel, the latter computed with the true parameter values. The relative absolute errors between the true and estimated memory kernels are within
\displaystyle 0.1
across periods
\displaystyle t\in[0.001,10]
.

.

6
Conclusions and Future Directions

We have studied parameter inference procedures for the highly degenerate class of SDEs
that includes a wide range of practical models, e.g., quasi-Markovian generalised equations (QGLEs),
epidemiological models with time-varying parameters
[
34
,
11
]
, non-linear continuous-time autoregressive models
[
35
]
and the classical Lorentz system upon consideration of noise effects
[
7
]
. We have introduced the locally Gaussian time-discretisation scheme (
LG-II
) and provided analytical/numerical results showcasing that parameter estimation based upon such scheme sidesteps biases that would arise under alternative schemes. The approach followed in this work for establishing our results for class (
Hypo-II
) are expected to also
guide extensions to more general classes of degenerate diffusions, for which iterated Lie brackets of
any
order, i.e.,
\displaystyle[\tilde{V}_{0},[\tilde{V}_{0},\ldots,[\tilde{V}_{0},V_{k}]]]
,
\displaystyle 1\leq k\leq d
, are required for Hormander’s condition to hold. Here, we draw upon the understanding obtained via the study of classes (
Hypo-I
) and (
Hypo-II
) to summarise key arguments for carrying out unbiased parameter estimation for general hypo-elliptic systems.
First, in a partial observation regime, use of a degenerate discretisation (e.g. Euler-Maruyama) or equivalently of finite-differences to impute latent components will induce bias at estimates of diffusion coefficient parameters (recall the case study in Section
4.2.1
). Also, note that finite-differences can be used only when the smooth component is determined as
\displaystyle dX_{S,t}=X_{R,t}dt
. Thus, a natural approach for parametric inference of degenerate diffusions is to develop a non-degenerate conditionally Gaussian scheme for the full coordinates, with the Gaussian noise obtained via high-order stochastic Taylor expansion of the drift functions.
A lot of care should be given at the deterministic terms of the expansion to be included into the scheme, to avoid emergence of biases in estimates of drift parameters (recall the analytical study in Section
4.1.3
and the numerical results in Section
5.1
).
We summarise below the above-designated roadmap for the construction of ‘correct’ time-discretisation schemes for general classes of hypo-elliptic diffusions.

Step 1.

For the rough component,
\displaystyle X_{R}
, the Euler-Maruyama scheme is applied.

Step 2.

For the smooth coordinates in the model, one recursively applies stochastic Taylor expansion to drift functions so that Gaussian variates, in the form of iterated integrals involving Brownian motions, e.g. of the form
\displaystyle\textstyle{\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}B_{s}ds}
,
\displaystyle\textstyle{\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\int_{t_{i}}^{u}B_{s}dsdu}
, appear in all smooth coordinates. This process is completed once the covariance-variance of the Gaussian approximation is positive definite.

Step 3.

For a smooth component containing Gaussian noise of size
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(\Delta_{n}^{(2k-1)/2})
,
\displaystyle k\geq 2
, the scheme should include all deterministic terms from the stochastic Taylor expansion up to size
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(\Delta_{n}^{k})
.

Indicatively, Table
6
summarises the size of determistic and noisy parts of the locally Gaussian scheme (
LG-II
) for class (
Hypo-II
).

Our work in this paper leads to further research in several directions.
In the CLT of the main analytical result for the parameter estimator (Theorem
2
), the step-size
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}
is required to satisfy
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}=o(n^{-1/2})
. An open problem for hypo-elliptic diffusions is the construction of estimators giving a CLT under a weaker condition
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}=o(n^{-1/p})
,
\displaystyle p\geq 3
.
We expect that such a general estimator for degenerate diffusion models can be produced, with accompanying theory then following the strategy used in our proofs in this work, as we mentioned in Remark
5
, that is, the proof of consistency does not require a condition of
\displaystyle\Delta_{n}=o(n^{-1/2})
. In a different direction, the effectiveness of the developed locally Gaussian scheme is yet to be studied under a low-frequency observation setting, i.e. with the step-size
\displaystyle\Delta
assumed fixed and not small enough, in which case a number, say
\displaystyle M
, of inner sub-steps are introduced by the user. Under such a setting, the discretisation error of the true (intractable) density over the period of size
\displaystyle\Delta
typically diminishes as
\displaystyle M
increases. In the case of elliptic diffusions, explicit rates of convergence to zero are provided in
[
16
,
19
]
. Finally,
in this work, in the practical scenario of partial observations, we have investigated the behaviour of discretisation schemes via case studies and numerical experiments. Analytical theory would be quite instructive in this setting. Techniques used in the context of hidden Markov models (see e.g.
[
10
]
) are expected to be valuable in such a pursuit.

\displaystyle\Delta_{n}

).
